### Tool result

_source line 13 · 2026-05-16T14:51:42.030Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BwJUxF41jrsVJTxnAc3ZS5`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Training-free LLM information extraction degrades as schema complexity grows. We present the first study to formally isolate type confusability from type count via stratified controlled design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE (a closed-set classification task), and event detection across four datasets, introducing the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) and two paradigms: count curves varying schema size while randomizing confusability, and BCS-controlled experiments fixing size while varying confusability.
3	
4	We identify two empirically separable degradation channels: count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type substitution. For RE, the quartile BCS gap is $-$5.3\,pp (95\% CI: [$-$7.4, $-$3.2]); typical random batches differ by ${\approx}\,2.2$\,pp. Under extreme-batch construction (a methodological upper-bound), the gap reaches 31.2\,pp. Batch-level BCS explains $R^2 = 0.22\text{--}0.53$ of accuracy variance as an aggregated signal, while pairwise effect sizes remain modest ($r^2 = 1.3\text{--}1.7\%$). The effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts. Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B; Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER. Count increases generally suppress extraction on cross-domain schemas; MAVEN's same-domain schema exhibits centrality confounding that reverses this trend. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers count-based degradation for RE (+29.8\,pp over single-pass), but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant additional gain (+1.4\,pp, $p\approx 0.19$).
5	\end{abstract}
6	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Training-free\footnote{We use ``training-free'' and ``zero-shot'' interchangeably to denote prompting without parameter updates.} LLM information extraction---prompting a large language model with a target schema and asking it to extract structured information without any task-specific fine-tuning---enables rapid deployment across new domains and ontologies. This zero-shot paradigm eliminates the need for labeled data, making it attractive for applications where annotation is expensive or schemas evolve frequently \cite{llm4ie-survey-2024}. Yet the paradigm carries a critical limitation: performance degrades sharply as the number of candidate types in the prompt schema grows. On named entity recognition, recall drops from 0.72 with a single candidate type to 0.42 with 64 types, a 42\% relative decline. On relation extraction, accuracy falls from 85.7\% with one relation to 50.4\% with 80 relations (sentence-level) and from 88.0\% to 38.7\% with 96 relations (document-level)---near or exceeding halving. This degradation is not academic: real-world ontologies routinely define 50--200+ types, placing them squarely in the regime where training-free extraction becomes unreliable.
5	
6	What causes this degradation? Prior decomposition strategies \cite{uniner-2024,llm-pee-2025,parse-2025} conflate two mechanisms: having more types to discriminate among (count) and having semantically similar types to confuse (confusability) (hereafter Channel~1 and Channel~2). None isolates which factor predicts the gains (\S\ref{sec:related_work}).
7	
8	We ask three questions. \textbf{RQ1}: Does type confusability independently predict extraction degradation beyond the effect of type count? \textbf{RQ2}: Through what mechanisms do count and confusability each degrade performance? \textbf{RQ3}: Can confusability-aware schema decomposition improve extraction quality?
9	
10	We present the first study to formally isolate type confusability from type count via stratified controlled design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE, and event detection (ED). We operationalize confusability via the \emph{Schema Confusability Score} (SCS)---pairwise embedding cosine similarity---and its batch-level aggregation, the \emph{Batch Confusability Score} (BCS). We evaluate on Few-NERD \cite{fewnerd-2021}, FewRel \cite{fewrel-2018}, DocRED \cite{docred-2019}, and MAVEN \cite{maven-2020} using Qwen3-32B (\S\ref{sec:experimental_setup}).
11	
12	Our experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two empirically separable channels---a confusability channel with a typical-batch gap of ${\approx}\,2.2$\,pp (quartile $\Delta = -5.3$\,pp, 95\% CI: [$-$7.4, $-$3.2]; up to 31.2\,pp under extreme-batch construction), and a count channel associated with recall decline up to $-$49\,pp; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification, but RE batch composition yields a directional +1.4\,pp gain.
13	
14	Our contributions are:
15	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt,topsep=2pt]
16	    \item \textbf{Controlled evidence for two degradation channels.} Count and confusability constitute mechanistically distinguishable dimensions: count predicts recall decline while confusability predicts type substitution, with differential error signatures (\S\ref{sec:multi_channel}).
17	    \item \textbf{Pairwise confusability as predictor.} SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$) while centrality confounds per-type aggregation on homogeneous schemas.
18	    \item \textbf{Cross-task BCS-controlled experiments.} BCS--accuracy slopes are consistent across schema sizes ($p = 0.60$) and replicate at document level (DocRED: $R^2 = 0.53$).
19	    \item \textbf{Diagnosis to mitigation.} Schema decomposition proves structurally infeasible for NER (merge amplification collapses precision from 0.56 to 0.21) and yields no statistically significant improvement for RE (graph-coloring: +1.4\,pp, $p\approx 0.19$). Oracle routing (+29.8\,pp) confirms the performance ceiling exists but highlights the gap between theoretical and achievable gains.
20	\end{enumerate}
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	Unless otherwise noted, all experiments in this section use the v1 prompt (\S\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).
5	
6	\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}
7	\label{sec:count_curves}
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	\centering
11	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\columnwidth}
12	\centering
13	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}
14	\end{minipage}
15	\hfill
16	\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\columnwidth}
17	\centering
18	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}
19	\end{minipage}
20	\caption{\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves across four datasets. NER recall generally declines and RE accuracy (both FewRel and DocRED) declines monotonically with schema size; MAVEN relaxed F1 increases (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). \textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}
21	\label{fig:main_results}
22	\end{figure}
23	
24	Figure~\ref{fig:main_results}a shows extraction quality degrades monotonically with schema size across three datasets: Few-NERD recall drops from 0.72 to 0.42 ($-$42\%), FewRel accuracy from 85.7\% to 50.4\% ($-$35.3\,pp), and DocRED from 88.0\% to 38.7\% ($-$56\%). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty.
25	
26	\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}
27	\label{sec:pairwise}
28	
29	We test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate---the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$---and correlate it with $\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\% CIs.
30	
31	\begin{table}[t]
32	\centering
33	\small
34	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
35	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}
36	\toprule
37	\textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{Embed.} & \textbf{$n$ pairs} & \textbf{$r$} & \textbf{$r^2$} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{$p$} \\
38	\midrule
39	Few-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
40	MAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\,0.001$ \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular}}
43	\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). We omit FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \S\ref{sec:scs-validation}) as RE's per-instance format precludes direct cross-task comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]---both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}
44	\label{tab:pairwise}
45	\end{table}
46	
47	The correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \approx 1.3\text{--}1.7\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\rho = 0.144\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\rho = 0.27\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).
48	
49	The remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\,pp under typical batching (\S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}).
50	
51	\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}
52	\label{sec:bcs_controlled}
53	
54	We fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\S\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.
55	
56	In BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\Delta=-5.3$\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\,=\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.
57	
58	\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}
59	The effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.
60	
61	\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}
62	\label{sec:within_pool}
63	Within-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \approx 0.60$; Appendix~\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.
64	
65	\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}
66	\label{sec:practical_magnitude}
67	For representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\,pp (95\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\% vs.\ 78.9\% for top-quartile ($\Delta = 3.9$\,pp). The 31.2\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Pair-level $R^2 \approx 7\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\ $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\%$ across all pairs; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \approx 22\%$ ($\sim$3$\times$ amplification, reproduced by Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation-sim}).
68	
69	\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}
70	The effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\approx$51\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\S\ref{sec:count_curves}); Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).
71	
72	\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}
73	Under the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ high-BCS 59.7\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$)---modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).
74	
75	\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}
76	\label{sec:multi_channel}
77	
78	\begin{figure}[t]
79	\centering
80	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}
81	\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\%) and non-monotonic.}
82	\label{fig:error_taxonomy}
83	\end{figure}
84	
85	Error taxonomy decomposition (Table~\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\text{--}27.5\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.
86	
87	These results establish two channels: \textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), cleanly separable in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact.
88	
89	\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}
90	\label{sec:centrality_confound}
91	
92	Per-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.
```

Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}).\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, reproduced by Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$)---modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), cleanly separable in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact.\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

```text
213	\section{Prompt Version Sensitivity Analysis}
214	\label{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}
215	
216	We assess whether the confusability effects reported in \S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}--\ref{sec:multi_channel} are robust to prompt design by replicating key experiments under the v2 prompt (\S\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).
217	
218	\paragraph{RE (FewRel).}
219	Under the v1 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$31.2\,pp (Table~\ref{tab:bcs_controlled}). Under v2, with $n{=}25$ batches per group and wider BCS contrast (low-BCS range [0.356, 0.367]; high-BCS range [0.791, 0.811]), the gap is $-$25.5\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ high-BCS 59.7\%; 95\% CI: [22.4, 28.6]; $p < 0.001$; Pearson $r = -0.919$). The effect is significant, directionally consistent with v1, and only modestly attenuated in magnitude ($-$5.7\,pp reduction). An earlier $n{=}5$ evaluation with narrower BCS contrast (0.21) yielded a smaller gap of $-$7.9\,pp (CI: [$-$5.2, $+$21.5]), illustrating how limited statistical power and restricted BCS range can obscure the underlying signal---a cautionary note for small-sample prompt sensitivity studies.
220	
221	\paragraph{NER (Few-NERD).}
222	The batch-level BCS--F1 correlation under v2 is consistent with v1: at count${}\,=\,12$, $r = +0.58$ ($p = 0.002$, 95\% CI: [0.19, 0.81]) vs.\ v1 $r = +0.50$ ($p = 0.008$). As discussed in \S\ref{sec:pairwise}, this positive direction reflects a type-frequency confound at the batch level---high-BCS batches contain high-frequency entity types that models extract more reliably---rather than evidence against the confusability hypothesis. The pairwise SCS--confusion analysis (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}), which is immune to this aggregation artifact, remains the primary evidence for the NER confusability channel.
223	
224	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
225	The $n{=}25$ v2 result confirms that the confusability effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude: the gap narrows ($-$5.7\,pp) but remains highly significant ($p < 0.001$, $r = -0.919$). The earlier $n{=}5$ non-significance reflected insufficient power and narrow BCS contrast, not absence of the effect. V2's stricter format constraints raise the baseline accuracy (low-BCS 85.2\% vs.\ 82.3\%), but type confusion under high confusability persists (59.7\% vs.\ 51.0\%). Prompt design modulates the practical magnitude of the confusability effect but does not eliminate it, ruling out prompt-specific confounding as the primary driver. The underlying confusability signal---pairwise SCS--confusion correlation and the directional BCS--accuracy relationship---persists across both prompt versions.
226	
227	\section{BCS Aggregation Sensitivity}
228	\label{sec:appendix_bcs_sensitivity}
229	
230	We define Batch Confusability Score (BCS) as the \emph{mean} pairwise SCS across all type pairs in a batch (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}). An alternative definition takes the \emph{maximum} pairwise SCS within each batch, capturing the single most confusable pair. Here we compare both aggregation methods to verify that core conclusions are robust to this choice.

244	MAX & 80-pool 16-sub & $-$0.401 & [$-$0.701, $-$0.069] & $-$0.221 & 0.027 \\
245	\bottomrule

247	\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \approx 0.60$; MAX $p \approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}
248	\label{tab:bcs_aggregation}
249	\end{table}
250	
251	MAX aggregation yields stronger correlations ($r = -0.637$ vs.\ $-0.467$ for 16-rel), consistent with the hypothesis that the single most confusable pair in a batch predicts substitution errors. Both definitions produce significant negative slopes across all conditions, and the permutation tests confirm no significant difference between independent and sampled pools under either definition. We adopt MEAN as the primary definition because it better represents average pairwise difficulty across all schema combinations, while MAX may overweight a single outlier pair. The choice does not affect any paper conclusion.
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}
5	The two-channel decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas (e.g., MAVEN's FrameNet-derived event types) exhibit centrality confounding that obscures per-type aggregated signals, requiring pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). High-BCS accuracy converges to $\approx$51\% across tested models, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty ceiling. RE's clean channel separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\,pp vs.\ v1 extreme-batch gap; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Why does embedding similarity predict LLM confusion?}
8	Type name embeddings capture semantic proximity in the same representational space that LLMs use for in-context type resolution. When two type names are close in embedding space, we hypothesize that the model's attention mechanism may produce overlapping key--query representations for those types, increasing the probability of substitution errors. This mechanistic link explains why a simple cosine similarity metric predicts extraction failures: high SCS directly indexes the degree of representational overlap that causes confusion during decoding. Critically, the pair-level effect is modest ($R^2 \approx 7\%$ over non-zero confusion pairs) because individual substitution events are stochastic and influenced by many factors beyond type similarity. However, pairs sharing a common type exhibit correlated SCS values ($\rho = 0.35$), so when multiple high-SCS pairs co-occur in a batch, their effects aggregate constructively: noise averages out while the confusability signal is preserved. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that this inter-pair correlation produces an effective amplification of ${\sim}3\times$, yielding batch-level $R^2 \approx 22\%$ from pair-level $R^2 \approx 7\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation-sim}). This aggregation mechanism explains why weak individual pair effects produce strong batch-level degradation---and why BCS (the mean pairwise SCS) is a useful schema-level diagnostic despite the modest pairwise signal. The modest pairwise effect is expected: individual substitution events are noisy and multi-causal. The practical contribution of SCS lies at the schema level, where batch-level BCS explains 22--53\% of accuracy variance---sufficient for pre-hoc schema screening.
9	
10	\paragraph{Schema design implications.}
11	Our findings suggest a pre-hoc workflow for practitioners designing extraction schemas. Before deployment, one can compute all-pairs SCS across the type inventory and flag pairs exceeding a high percentile (e.g., 75th) of the corpus's SCS distribution as high-risk for mutual confusion. Three mitigation strategies follow from the two-channel decomposition:
12	(i)~\emph{Expand definitions}---for high-SCS pairs, include contrastive definitions or disambiguating examples in the prompt to increase representational separation;
13	(ii)~\emph{Rename types}---replace semantically proximate labels with more descriptive alternatives (e.g., ``person-artist'' $\to$ ``visual artist (painter/sculptor)'') to reduce embedding overlap;
14	(iii)~\emph{Decompose batches}---assign high-SCS pairs to different extraction passes using graph coloring on the SCS-thresholded confusability graph (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}), accepting the precision--recall trade-off that merge introduces.
15	We deliberately avoid recommending a fixed SCS threshold, as optimal granularity is task-dependent: the acceptable confusion rate depends on downstream costs, schema size, and available compute budget for multi-pass extraction.
16	
17	\paragraph{Cross-domain connections.}
18	Schema complexity effects may parallel findings in schema-guided dialogue systems (\S2; see Appendix~\ref{app:cross-domain} for discussion).
19	
20	\paragraph{Model scale and confusability sensitivity.}
21	Preliminary evidence from two model sizes (32B vs.\ 8B) suggests that Channel~2 (confusability) effects attenuate on smaller models due to floor effects, while Channel~1 (count) replicates fully (Tables~\ref{tab:cross_model}--\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Systematic scaling studies remain future work.
22	
23	\paragraph{Future directions.}
24	Three directions merit investigation: (i)~non-oracle routing strategies that make decomposition deployable without gold labels; (ii)~broader model coverage, particularly proprietary APIs; and (iii)~definition-inclusive SCS (current name+definition ablation shows no systematic improvement; Appendix~\ref{app:scs-ablation}). For NER, calibration-aware merge or joint decoding may address merge amplification (\S\ref{sec:sdgd}).
25	
26	\section{Conclusion}
27	\label{sec:conclusion}
28	
29	We isolated schema-induced degradation into count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type confusion, showed these channels are separable in RE and replicate across model families (Llama-3.1-8B: count $r = 0.725$, confusability $\Delta = -27$\,pp) and extraction granularities (DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$). We further identified a centrality confound on same-domain schemas and demonstrated that merge amplification is a structural barrier to NER decomposition, while RE batch composition yields a directional +1.4\,pp gain ($p\approx 0.19$). Cross-batch calibration and broader model validation are key next steps.
30	
```

</details>

```json
      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary conditions.}\nThe two-channel decomposition applies to cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas (e.g., MAVEN's FrameNet-derived event types) exhibit centrality confounding that obscures per-type aggregated signals, requiring pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). High-BCS accuracy converges to $\\approx$51\\% across tested models, suggesting a task-inherent difficulty ceiling. RE's clean channel separation may partly reflect its closed-set format; the effect generalizes to document-level RE (DocRED slope $-$0.71). Prompt design modulates but does not eliminate the effect (v2 gap: $-$25.5\\,pp vs.\\ v1 extreme-batch gap; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why does embedding similarity predict LLM confusion?}\nType name embeddings capture semantic proximity in the same representational space that LLMs use for in-context type resolution. When two type names are close in embedding space, we hypothesize that the model's attention mechanism may produce overlapping key--query representations for those types, increasing the probability of substitution errors. This mechanistic link explains why a simple cosine similarity metric predicts extraction failures: high SCS directly indexes the degree of representational overlap that causes confusion during decoding. Critically, the pair-level effect is modest ($R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ over non-zero confusion pairs) because individual substitution events are stochastic and influenced by many factors beyond type similarity. However, pairs sharing a common type exhibit correlated SCS values ($\\rho = 0.35$), so when multiple high-SCS pairs co-occur in a batch, their effects aggregate constructively: noise averages out while the confusability signal is preserved. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that this inter-pair correlation produces an effective amplification of ${\\sim}3\\times$, yielding batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ from pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}). This aggregation mechanism explains why weak individual pair effects produce strong batch-level degradation---and why BCS (the mean pairwise SCS) is a useful schema-level diagnostic despite the modest pairwise signal. ```text
```
```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:experimental_setup}
3	
4	We evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).
5	
6	\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}
7	\label{sec:datasets}
8	
9	\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}
10	Few-NERD \cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances and evaluate with token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.
11	
12	\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}
13	FewRel \cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.
14	
15	\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}
16	DocRED \cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.
17	
18	\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}
19	MAVEN \cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.
20	
21	Table~\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \S\ref{sec:scs}.
22	
23	\begin{table}[t]
24	\centering
25	\small
26	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
27	\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}
28	\toprule
29	\textbf{Dataset} & \textbf{Task} & \textbf{\# Types} & \textbf{\# Eval Units} & \textbf{Domain} & \textbf{BCS Range} & \textbf{Embedding} \\
30	\midrule
31	Few-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\
32	FewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\
33	DocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\
34	MAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\rlap{$^\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\
35	\bottomrule
36	\end{tabular}}
37	\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}
38	\label{tab:datasets}
39	\end{table}
40	
41	\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}
42	\label{sec:scs}
43	
44	We introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\text{embed}(\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:
45	{\small
46	\[
47	\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \cos\bigl(\text{embed}(\text{name}(t_i)),\; \text{embed}(\text{name}(t_j))\bigr)
48	\]
49	}
50	
51	For a batch of types $B = \{t_1, \ldots, t_k\}$, the \textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:
52	\[
53	\text{BCS}(B) = \frac{1}{\binom{k}{2}} \sum_{i < j,\; t_i, t_j \in B} \text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)
54	\]
55	
56	We compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\,=\,0.034$ vs.\ MiniLM std${}\,=\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.
57	
58	\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}
59	\label{sec:scs-validation}
60	SCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on Few-NERD ($r = 0.112$, $p < 0.001$) and FewRel ($r = 0.187$, $p < 0.001$). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\S\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.
61	
62	\subsection{Experimental Design}
63	\label{sec:exp_design}
64	
65	We conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.
66	
67	\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}
68	We vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\} for Few-NERD, \{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\} for FewRel, \{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\} for DocRED, and \{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\} for MAVEN.
69	
70	\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}
71	We fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\,\in\{6, 12\}$), FewRel (count$\,\in\{8, 16\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\,\in\{16, 32\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).
72	
73	\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}
74	\label{sec:metrics}
75	
76	\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.
77	
78	
79	\subsection{Model and Inference}
80	\label{sec:model}
81	
82	We use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\,=\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).
83	
```

</details>

We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on Few-NERD ($r = 0.112$, $p < 0.001$) and FewRel ($r = 0.187$, $p < 0.001$). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).\n",
```json
      "numLines": 83,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 83
    }
  }
}
```

```text
301	
302	In the full 80-relation condition, natural BCS variation is insufficient (IQR${}=0.033$) to yield a detectable effect ($n=25$, slope${}=+0.221$, $p \approx 0.76$). This does not invalidate the confusability effect---the within-pool slope is significant ($p=0.027$) when BCS is deliberately varied---but establishes that natural schema sampling at this scale does not produce sufficient BCS contrast for detection.
303	
304	\section{SCS Embedding Ablation: Name-Only vs. Name+Definition}
305	\label{app:scs-ablation}
306	
307	Table~\ref{tab:scs-ablation} compares predictive accuracy of name-only SCS against name+definition SCS across three datasets.
308	
309	\begin{table}[h]
310	\centering
311	\small
312	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
313	\toprule
314	Dataset & SCS$_\text{name}$ $r$ & SCS$_\text{def}$ $r$ & $\Delta r$ & Matrix corr. \\

319	\bottomrule
320	\end{tabular}
321	\caption{Pearson correlation between SCS and empirical confusion rate. ``Def'' concatenates type name with definition before embedding. All correlations significant ($p < 0.001$). The inconsistent direction of $\Delta r$ indicates no systematic benefit from including definitions.}
322	\label{tab:scs-ablation}
323	\end{table}
324	
325	Few-NERD is the only dataset whose prompts include type definitions; for MAVEN and FewRel, prompts use type names alone, making name-only SCS a direct match to model input. The high matrix correlation (0.66--0.89) confirms that type names capture the dominant semantic structure.
326	
327	\section{Pair-to-Batch Aggregation Simulation}
328	\label{app:aggregation-sim}
329	
330	We verify that the pair-level $R^2 \approx 7\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \approx 22\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation.
331	
332	\paragraph{Setup.} From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\rho_\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.
333	
334	\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\sim$3$\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\approx 3.4$, not 28).
335	
336	\section{Cross-Domain Connections}
337	\label{app:cross-domain}
338	
339	The schema-induced degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-channel decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole driver; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications---a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics---and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.
340	
341	\end{document}
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Training-free LLM IE.}
5	Training-free LLM IE spans instruction tuning \cite{instructuie-2023}, code-style schema encoding \cite{knowcoder-2024,codeie-2023}, annotation-guided extraction \cite{gollie-2024,adelie-2024}, schema-driven decoding \cite{slot-2025,oneke-2025}, and constrained JSON decoding \cite{json-schema-bench-2025}. For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.
6	
7	\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}
8	The most directly related work is LLM-PEE \cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification---not partitioning quality---is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms.
9	
10	\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion.
11	
12	\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition---pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings---with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work.
13	
14	\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}
15	Our centrality confound (\S\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.
16	
17	
```

For NER, methods include zero-shot decomposition \\cite{xie-zeroshot-ner-2023,promptner-2023}, distillation \\cite{uniner-2024}, and bidirectional encoders \\cite{gliner-2024,gliner2-2025}. For RE, recent work examines zero-shot relation triplet extraction via prompting \\cite{relationprompt-2022} and revisits relation extraction in the LLM era \\cite{wadhwa-re-llm-2023}. These methods perform well with moderate schemas (4--20 types) but none investigates how performance degrades as schema complexity scales, nor proposes quantitative metrics for inter-type confusability.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in IE.}\nThe most directly related work is LLM-PEE \\cite{llm-pee-2025}, which partitions large event schemas by type embedding similarity. Our work differs in three ways: (1)~we conduct controlled analysis to understand \\emph{why} extraction degrades rather than developing an engineering solution; (2)~our BCS-controlled experiments isolate count from confusability, which LLM-PEE conflates; (3)~our SDGD experiments reveal that merge amplification---not partitioning quality---is the bottleneck in decompose-then-merge pipelines. Other approaches address schema complexity via hard negative mining \\cite{iepile-2024}, schema definition optimization \\cite{parse-2025}, multi-agent debate \\cite{crossagentie-2025,cmas-ner-2025}, schema-aware decomposition for event extraction \\cite{dee-2024}, and active prompting \\cite{reflect-learn-2026}; all treat it as an engineering problem rather than isolating the underlying degradation mechanisms.\n\n\\paragraph{Schema complexity in dialogue and IE.} The Schema-Guided Dialogue dataset~\\cite{rastogi2020towards} and SGD-X~\\cite{lee2022sgd} document performance degradation with schema linguistic variation. \\citet{li2023evaluating} observe count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT IE. \\citet{volkanovska2024ner} categorize LLM NER errors including type confusion.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS novelty.} SCS shares its formal definition---pairwise cosine similarity of type name embeddings---with the partitioning criterion used in LLM-PEE~\\cite{llm-pee-2025} for schema decomposition. Our contribution is not the metric itself but the controlled experimental framework that isolates confusability's predictive relationship with extraction degradation, distinguishing it from the engineering use in prior work.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding anisotropy.}\nOur centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}) connects to representation degeneration: embeddings collapse into narrow cones \\cite{gao-repr-degen-2019,ethayarajh-contextual-2019}, but local clusters can be isotropic \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}---explaining why MAVEN's single-ontology types conflate confusability with centrality.\n\n",
```json
      "numLines": 17,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 17
    }
  }
}
```

```text
35	
36	\input{discussion}
37	
38	\section*{Limitations}
39	\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=2pt]
40	\item \textbf{Model coverage.} Primary experiments use Qwen3-32B with cross-model validation on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Whether patterns hold for proprietary models (GPT-4, Gemini) remains unverified. Channel~2 was not robustly detectable on NER at 8B scale, likely due to floor effects.
41	
42	\item \textbf{Modest pairwise effect size.} Pairwise SCS explains 1.3--1.7\% of type confusion variance; entity frequency, prompt position, mention ambiguity, and type definition quality likely account for the remainder.
43	
44	\item \textbf{Non-significant factorial test.} Within-dataset count $\times$ BCS interaction on Few-NERD did not reach significance (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}); cross-pool slope consistency suggests confusability sensitivity is stable rather than amplifying.
45	
46	\item \textbf{Decomposition and metric scope.} RE batch composition is tested on FewRel only; NER merge alternatives remain unexplored. SCS relies on embedding cosine similarity; label entropy or ontological depth may capture complementary dimensions.
47	
48	\item \textbf{RE format specificity.} RE's clean channel separation may reflect its closed-set classification format. FewRel combines training and validation splits for 80 types and characterizes classification accuracy rather than end-to-end extraction.
49	
50	\item \textbf{Causal mechanism.} The study establishes correlational evidence with controlled stratification; mechanistic analysis at the attention or logit level is needed to confirm the causal pathway. The controlled stratification provides evidence consistent with a directional relationship but does not establish interventional causality.
51	
52	\item \textbf{Data contamination.} FewRel (2018) may appear in pretraining corpora. However, train-origin relations show numerically lower accuracy than val-origin ($-8.8$\,pp, $p=0.06$, n.s.), opposite to the contamination hypothesis. Moreover, the monotonic count-dependent degradation curve (85.7\%$\to$50.4\%) cannot be explained by uniform contamination, which would elevate all conditions equally.
53	\end{enumerate}
54	
55	\paragraph{Multiple comparisons.} We do not apply family-wise correction across datasets, treating each as an independent replication. Within-dataset secondary analyses are reported with individual $p$-values; core claims rest on effects reaching $p < 0.001$.
56	
57	\input{checklist}
58	
59	\bibliography{references}
60	
61	\appendix
62	
63	\section{Additional Figures}
64	\label{sec:appendix_figures}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013g1mYmWfJL3XhjUajbdQzf`</summary>

```text
150	
151	Decomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.
152	
153	Our v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\_zero: 0.210$\to$0.324; random\_batch: 0.249$\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\to$0.191; 0.446$\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\_zero: 0.251$\to$0.241; random\_batch: 0.320$\to$0.301).
154	
155	\section{Factorial Analysis}
156	\label{sec:appendix_factorial}
157	
158	We conducted a within-dataset $2\!\times\!2$ factorial analysis (count $\times$ BCS) on Few-NERD to test whether the two degradation channels exhibit a statistical interaction. The interaction term was directionally consistent but not statistically significant ($z = -0.67$, $p = 0.50$), likely underpowered due to BCS range compression in high-count schemas. Cross-pool slope analysis on FewRel (\S\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) shows consistent BCS--accuracy sensitivity across pool sizes (permutation $p \approx 0.60$), suggesting that the per-unit confusability effect is stable rather than amplifying with schema size. A formal factorial test on FewRel was not conducted because the BCS-controlled design varies confusability within fixed count levels rather than crossing both factors.
159	
160	\section{Within-Pool CI Details}
161	\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}
162	
163	The quartile $\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\,pp, 95\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.
164	
165	\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}
166	\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}
167	
168	Section~\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.
169	
170	\paragraph{Confound identification.}
171	For each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:
172	\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]
173	\item \textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.
174	\item \textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors---central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.
175	\end{itemize}
176	
177	\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}
178	Table~\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.
179	
180	\begin{table}[h]
181	\centering
182	\small
183	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
184	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
185	\toprule
186	\textbf{Embedding} & \textbf{Control} & \textbf{Raw $\rho$} & \textbf{Raw $p$} & \textbf{Partial $r$} & \textbf{Partial $p$} \\
187	\midrule
188	MiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\
189	MiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\
190	MiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\
191	\midrule
192	Qwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\
193	Qwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\
194	Qwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\
195	\bottomrule
196	\end{tabular}}
197	\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \texttt{exp\_maven\_residualized\_bcs}.}
198	\label{tab:residualized_bcs}
199	\end{table}
200	
201	\paragraph{Interpretation.}
202	The two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality---the count of strong semantic neighbors---becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.
203	
204	Under joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.
205	
206	\section{Batch Composition Results}
207	\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}
208	
209	See Table~\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \S\ref{sec:sdgd}.
```

```json
      "content": "\nDecomposition boosts recall (per-type recall reaches 0.586 versus single-pass 0.368) but devastates precision (0.194 versus 0.563). When the same text is presented to the model $k$ times with different type subsets, each pass independently generates entity predictions. Because LLM outputs are not calibrated across passes, the union merge step accumulates overlapping and contradictory predictions, inflating false positives.\n\nOur v2 merge ranks competing predictions by the LLM's self-reported confidence and retains only the highest-scoring candidate for each span. Confidence gating improves precision (sdgd\\_zero: 0.210$\\to$0.324; random\\_batch: 0.249$\\to$0.331), but at the cost of disproportionate recall loss (0.314$\\to$0.191; 0.446$\\to$0.276), yielding lower F1 in both modes (sdgd\\_zero: 0.251$\\to$0.241; random\\_batch: 0.320$\\to$0.301).\n\n\\section{Factorial Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nWe conducted a within-dataset $2\\!\\times\\!2$ factorial analysis (count $\\times$ BCS) on Few-NERD to test whether the two degradation channels exhibit a statistical interaction. The interaction term was directionally consistent but not statistically significant ($z = -0.67$, $p = 0.50$), likely underpowered due to BCS range compression in high-count schemas. Cross-pool slope analysis on FewRel (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) shows consistent BCS--accuracy sensitivity across pool sizes (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$), suggesting that the per-unit confusability effect is stable rather than amplifying with schema size. A formal factorial test on FewRel was not conducted because the BCS-controlled design varies confusability within fixed count levels rather than crossing both factors.\n\n\\section{Within-Pool CI Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_within_pool}\n\nThe quartile $\\Delta$ for 80-pool 16-relation subsets ($-$3.92\\,pp, 95\\% CI [$-$8.17, $+$0.38]) crosses zero, reflecting the compressed BCS range within that pool (0.46--0.68 vs.\\ 0.48--0.71 for the independent pool). Within this narrow band, the quartile contrast lacks statistical power to detect the effect that the regression identifies. The full 80-relation condition ($n = 25$) is underpowered for both the slope (CI: [$-$1.303, $+$1.695]) and quartile analyses; we include it in Table~\\ref{tab:within_pool} for completeness but do not draw inferential conclusions from it.\n\n\\section{Controlling for Centrality Confounds in MAVEN Per-Type Analysis}\n\\label{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}\n\nSection~\\ref{sec:centrality_confound} identifies a centrality confound in MAVEN's per-type BCS--F1 correlation: both embeddings show a \\emph{positive} raw correlation, opposite to the expected direction. Here we formalize this analysis by identifying specific confounders for each embedding and testing whether the BCS--F1 relationship survives partial correlation controlling for these confounders.\n\n\\paragraph{Confound identification.}\nFor each embedding, we computed the correlation between mean BCS and candidate confounders (type frequency, degree centrality at threshold 0.7) across MAVEN's 149 active event types. The dominant confound differs by embedding:\n\\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{MiniLM}: BCS correlates with log type frequency ($r = 0.201$). Frequent types have more training signal and are extracted more reliably, producing the positive BCS--F1 association.\n\\item \\textbf{Qwen3}: BCS correlates strongly with degree centrality ($r = 0.854$). In the Qwen3 embedding space, high-BCS types are those with many semantic neighbors---central, well-represented types that the model extracts well.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Partial correlation results.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:residualized_bcs} reports raw Spearman $\\rho$ and partial Pearson $r$ after controlling for the identified confounders. Under single-variable control, both correlations weaken to non-significance. Under joint control (log frequency $+$ degree centrality simultaneously), neither embedding retains a significant BCS--F1 association.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Embedding} & \\textbf{Control} & \\textbf{Raw $\\rho$} & \\textbf{Raw $p$} & \\textbf{Partial $r$} & \\textbf{Partial $p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMiniLM & --- & $+$0.208 & $0.011$ & --- & --- \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq & --- & --- & $+$0.133 & $0.11$ \\\\\nMiniLM & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.23$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 & --- & $+$0.184 & $0.025$ & --- & --- \\\\\nQwen3 & degree centrality & --- & --- & $+$0.063 & $0.44$ \\\\\nQwen3 & log freq $+$ degree & --- & --- & --- & $0.44$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{MAVEN per-type BCS--F1 correlation before and after controlling for centrality confounders. Raw correlations are Spearman $\\rho$; partial correlations are Pearson $r$ from residualized regression. Degree centrality is computed on the SCS graph at threshold 0.7. All values from experiment \\texttt{exp\\_maven\\_residualized\\_bcs}.}\n\\label{tab:residualized_bcs}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThe two embeddings encode different aspects of the same underlying confound. MiniLM, a smaller sentence encoder, produces a tighter embedding cluster for MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types; here, type frequency is the primary confounder because frequent types accumulate higher mean similarity to the dense cluster. Qwen3, with a larger representation space, differentiates types enough that frequency is not the dominant factor, but degree centrality---the count of strong semantic neighbors---becomes the confound instead. In both cases, the positive raw correlation reflects a centrality artifact: types that are semantically central are both high-BCS and easy to extract.\n\nUnder joint control, both embeddings yield $p > 0.2$, confirming that the raw positive BCS--F1 correlation on MAVEN is fully attributable to centrality-related confounders. This does not invalidate the confusability hypothesis; the pairwise SCS--confusion correlation on MAVEN remains significant (MiniLM $r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}). Rather, it demonstrates that per-type aggregation (mean BCS) conflates confusability with centrality in semantically homogeneous schemas, and that pairwise analysis or explicit confound adjustment is required in such settings.\n\n\\section{Batch Composition Results}\n\\label{sec:appendix_batch_composition}\n\nSee Table~\\ref{tab:batch_composition} and the accompanying discussion in \\S\\ref{sec:sdgd}.",
```

```text
330	We verify that the pair-level $R^2 \approx 7\%$ (computed over non-zero confusion pairs) can aggregate to the observed batch-level $R^2 \approx 22\%$ via a Monte Carlo simulation.
331	
332	\paragraph{Setup.} From the FewRel 16-relation experiment, we estimate the inter-pair SCS correlation for pairs sharing a common type ($\rho_\text{SCS} = 0.35$). For batches of $k=8$ types ($\binom{8}{2}=28$ pairs), we simulate 10{,}000 iterations: (1) generate 28 correlated pair-level effects with individual $R^2 = 0.074$; (2) average to obtain batch-level signals; (3) compute batch-level $R^2$.
333	
334	\paragraph{Results.} Simulated batch $R^2 = 0.219$ [95\% CI: 0.092--0.361], matching the observed value of 0.218. The effective amplification factor is $\sim$3$\times$, explained by signal correlation within batches preserving variance while independent noise averages out (effective degrees of freedom $\approx 3.4$, not 28).
335	
336	\section{Cross-Domain Connections}
337	\label{app:cross-domain}
338	
339	The schema-induced degradation documented here is not unique to information extraction. Schema-Guided Dialogue systems (SGD/SGD-X; \citealt{rastogi2020towards}) face an analogous challenge: as the number of intent and slot types grows, dialogue state tracking accuracy declines. \citet{li2023evaluating} observed count-dependent degradation in ChatGPT-based IE, attributing it to prompt overload. Our two-channel decomposition offers a more refined explanation: schema size per se is not the sole driver; rather, larger schemas increase the probability of including high-SCS pairs, which contribute disproportionately to confusion. This distinction has practical implications---a schema of 80 types with low mutual confusability may outperform a 40-type schema with clustered semantics---and suggests that confusability-aware design principles may generalize to any task where an LLM must discriminate among a prompt-specified label inventory.
340	
341	\end{document}
342
```

```json
    "newString": "Under extreme-batch construction, the gap widens substantially (Table~3).",
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nTraining-free LLM information extraction degrades as schema complexity grows. We present the first study to formally isolate type confusability from type count via stratified controlled design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE (a closed-set classification task), and event detection across four datasets, introducing the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) and two paradigms: count curves varying schema size while randomizing confusability, and BCS-controlled experiments fixing size while varying confusability.\n\nWe identify two empirically separable degradation channels: count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type substitution. For RE, the quartile BCS gap is $-$5.3\\,pp (95\\% CI: [$-$7.4, $-$3.2]); typical random batches differ by ${\\approx}\\,2.2$\\,pp. Under extreme-batch construction (a methodological upper-bound), the gap reaches 31.2\\,pp. Batch-level BCS explains $R^2 = 0.22\\text{--}0.53$ of accuracy variance as an aggregated signal, while pairwise effect sizes remain modest ($r^2 = 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$). The effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts. Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B; Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER. Count increases generally suppress extraction on cross-domain schemas; MAVEN's same-domain schema exhibits centrality confounding that reverses this trend. We present the first study to formally isolate type confusability from type count via stratified controlled design in zero-shot LLM IE, spanning NER, RE (a closed-set classification task), and event detection across four datasets, introducing the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) and two paradigms: count curves varying schema size while randomizing confusability, and BCS-controlled experiments fixing size while varying confusability.",
```
