    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nUnless otherwise noted, all experiments in this section use the v1 prompt (\\S\\ref{sec:model}; full templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}).\n\n\\subsection{Count-Degradation Curves}\n\\label{sec:count_curves}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_count_curves.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\hfill\n\\begin{minipage}[t]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_bcs_scaling.pdf}\n\\end{minipage}\n\\caption{\\textbf{(a)} Count-degradation curves across four datasets. NER recall generally declines and RE accuracy (both FewRel and DocRED) declines monotonically with schema size; MAVEN relaxed F1 increases (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). \\textbf{(b)} BCS-controlled FewRel accuracy. The BCS--accuracy slope is consistent across the independent 16-relation pool and within-pool 16-relation subsets (permutation $p = 0.60$); the wider BCS range available at 80 relations produces larger absolute gaps.}\n\\label{fig:main_results}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:main_results}a shows extraction quality degrades monotonically with schema size across three datasets: Few-NERD recall drops from 0.72 to 0.42 ($-$42\\%), FewRel accuracy from 85.7\\% to 50.4\\% ($-$35.3\\,pp), and DocRED from 88.0\\% to 38.7\\% ($-$56\\%). MAVEN exhibits a reversed trend attributable to centrality confounding (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}). The effect replicates on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct ($-$43\\,pp on Few-NERD; per-batch $r = 0.725$; Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}), indicating schema configuration rather than model-specific factors predicts difficulty.\n\n\\subsection{Pairwise Confusability and Type Confusion}\n\\label{sec:pairwise}\n\nWe test whether pairwise SCS predicts type-level confusion rates. For each type pair $(t_i, t_j)$, we compute the confusion rate---the fraction of $t_i$ gold instances misclassified as $t_j$---and correlate it with $\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)$. Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise} reports Pearson correlations with bootstrap 95\\% CIs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Embed.} & \\textbf{$n$ pairs} & \\textbf{$r$} & \\textbf{$r^2$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & Qwen3 & 1{,}953 & 0.112 & 0.013 & {[}0.076, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\nMAVEN & MiniLM & 11{,}026 & 0.129 & 0.017 & {[}0.104, 0.156{]} & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Pairwise SCS--confusion correlation. $r^2$ indicates the fraction of variance in type confusion explained by embedding similarity. Both correlations are modest in magnitude but robust across datasets and embedding models (both $p \\ll 0.001$). Confusion rate for each ordered pair $(t_i, t_j)$ is computed as the total bidirectional confusion count $C(t_i{\\to}t_j) + C(t_j{\\to}t_i)$ divided by the sum of gold frequencies, aggregated over all count conditions; CIs are pair-level percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). We omit FewRel ($r = 0.187$; \\S\\ref{sec:scs-validation}) as RE's per-instance format precludes direct cross-task comparison of confusion rates. Type-level cluster bootstrap CIs: Few-NERD [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN [0.081, 0.185]---both exclude zero, confirming robustness to within-type correlation.}\n\\label{tab:pairwise}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe correlations are statistically significant but modest: $r^2 \\approx 1.3\\text{--}1.7\\%$. Type-level cluster bootstrap yields wider but still significant CIs (Few-NERD: [0.069, 0.219]; MAVEN: [0.081, 0.185]; $B = 10{,}000$). Both datasets show significant positive correlations between pairwise SCS and type confusion rates. Spearman correlations corroborate: $\\rho = 0.144\\text{--}0.227$ across all pairs, strengthening to $\\rho = 0.27\\text{--}0.31$ among pairs with nonzero confusion (though conditioning on the dependent variable may inflate this).\n\nThe remaining variance likely reflects entity frequency, prompt position \\cite{lost-in-middle-2024}, mention ambiguity, and definition quality. Although pairwise $r^2$ is modest, the signal compounds at the batch level into quartile gaps of 3.9--5.3\\,pp under typical batching (\\S\\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}).\n\n\\subsection{BCS-Controlled Experiments}\n\\label{sec:bcs_controlled}\n\nWe fix type count and vary BCS. FewRel's balanced design (700 instances per type) eliminates type frequency as a confound; the within-pool analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:within_pool}) controls for pool composition.\n\nIn BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\\Delta=-5.3$\\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\\,=\\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.\n\n\\paragraph{Document-level RE (DocRED).}\nThe effect replicates on DocRED's 96-relation schema: BCS gap $-$23.3\\,pp at count 16 (slope $= -0.713$, $\\rho = -0.661$, $p < 0.001$) and $-$17.6\\,pp at count 32 (slope $= -0.690$, $\\rho = -0.509$, $p = 0.002$). BCS explains 44--53\\% of cross-batch variance ($R^2 = 0.525$ at count 16), substantially higher than FewRel ($R^2 = 0.218$), possibly reflecting a wider confusability range in the DocRED schema, though document-level context may also provide richer cues for type confusion. Across all RE settings, BCS slopes range from $-$0.40 to $-$0.71, significant in all conditions with sufficient BCS range.\n\n\\paragraph{Within-pool controlled comparison.}\n\\label{sec:within_pool}\nWithin-pool analysis confirms slope consistency across pools (permutation $p \\approx 0.60$; Appendix~\\ref{app:within-pool-method}). The 80-relation extreme-batch gap (\\S\\ref{sec:bcs_controlled}) reflects wider BCS range, not stronger per-unit sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical magnitude.}\n\\label{sec:practical_magnitude}\nFor representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\\,pp (95\\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\\% vs.\\ 78.9\\% for top-quartile ($\\Delta = 3.9$\\,pp). The 31.2\\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Pair-level $R^2 \\approx 7\\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\\ $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\\%$ across all pairs; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \\approx 22\\%$ ($\\sim$3$\\times$ amplification, reproduced by Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation-sim}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model validation.}\nThe effect replicates on Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct: BCS gaps of $-$27.1\\,pp (count 8) and $-$23.9\\,pp (count 16), reaching 77--87\\% of Qwen3-32B's gap. Both models converge to $\\approx$51\\% under high confusability regardless of count, while divergence is confined to the low-BCS regime---suggesting a task-inherent difficulty floor under high confusability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model_bcs}). Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B (\\S\\ref{sec:count_curves}); Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER (Limitations, item~1).\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt sensitivity.}\nUnder the v2 prompt, the extreme-batch BCS gap is $-$25.5\\,pp (low-BCS 85.2\\% vs.\\ high-BCS 59.7\\%; $n{=}25$; $r = -0.919$; $p < 0.001$)---modestly attenuated from v1's extreme-batch gap but highly significant. NER v2 yields consistent batch-level correlations ($r = +0.58$ vs.\\ $r = +0.50$ under v1). Prompt design modulates magnitude but does not eliminate the effect (Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompt_sensitivity}).\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Channel Degradation}\n\\label{sec:multi_channel}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{../../figures/paper/fig_error_taxonomy.pdf}\n\\caption{Error taxonomy decomposition. IntraSub scales monotonically with BCS in both tasks. RE missed rates remain flat (10--16\\%), but NER missed rates are substantially higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic.}\n\\label{fig:error_taxonomy}\n\\end{figure}\n\nError taxonomy decomposition (Table~\\ref{tab:error_taxonomy} and Figure~\\ref{fig:error_taxonomy}) shows intra-batch substitution (IntraSub) scales monotonically with BCS: $\\rho = 1.0$ ($p < 0.001$) for NER and $\\rho = 0.954$ ($p < 0.001$) for RE. Under closed-set classification, non-\\texttt{none} errors are necessarily intra-batch; the more informative finding is that IntraSub confusion frequency correlates with pairwise SCS ($\\rho{=}0.954$, $p{<}0.001$). In NER, missed rates are higher (43--65\\%) and non-monotonic, indicating confusable types impair entity detection itself. Extreme-BCS batches produce $9.9\\text{--}27.5\\times$ more confusion than low-BCS batches.\n\nThese results establish two channels: \\textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \\textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), cleanly separable in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact.\n\n\\subsection{Centrality Confound on Homogeneous Schemas}\n\\label{sec:centrality_confound}\n\nPer-type analysis on MAVEN reveals an anomalous \\emph{positive} BCS--F1 correlation (Qwen3 $\\rho = +0.184$, $p = 0.025$; MiniLM $\\rho = +0.208$, $p = 0.011$). This traces to same-domain embedding collapse \\cite{cai-isotropy-clusters-2021}: MAVEN's FrameNet-derived types cluster tightly, so mean BCS conflates confusability with centrality---high-BCS types are semantically central and easy to extract. Controlling for log frequency and degree centrality, the positive correlation vanishes ($p > 0.2$; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_residualized_bcs}). The pairwise SCS signal remains intact ($r = 0.129$, $p < 0.001$; Table~\\ref{tab:pairwise}); the problem is per-type aggregation, not the underlying relationship. Few-NERD's cross-domain schema does not exhibit this confound ($\\rho = -0.293$, $p = 0.02$). Mean BCS is thus reliable only for cross-domain schemas; same-domain schemas require pairwise SCS or centrality adjustment.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances and evaluate with token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on Few-NERD ($r = 0.112$, $p < 0.001$) and FewRel ($r = 0.187$, $p < 0.001$). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).\n",

81

RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).

{

  "session_id": "2a15256a-5c69-4223-86ab-512a1ea1a1e9",

  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex",
    "oldString": "Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).",
    "newString": "Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation; variance across seeds is not quantified. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances uniformly across test instances; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on Few-NERD ($r = 0.112$, $p < 0.001$) and FewRel ($r = 0.187$, $p < 0.001$). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).\n",

Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "type": "user",
  "parent_tool_use_id": null,
  "session_id": "2a15256a-5c69-4223-86ab-512a1ea1a1e9",

  "tool_use_result": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_18/projects/schema-dag-extraction/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex",
    "oldString": "All experiments use single-seed evaluation; variance across seeds is not quantified. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).",
    "newString": "All experiments use single-seed evaluation; variance across seeds is not quantified. Pair-level CIs use standard percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$ due to computational cost.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experimental_setup}\n\nWe evaluate how prompt schema size and inter-type confusability affect zero-shot LLM extraction across three information extraction tasks and four datasets: named entity recognition (NER), sentence-level relation extraction (RE), document-level RE, and event detection (ED).\n\n\\subsection{Tasks and Datasets}\n\\label{sec:datasets}\n\n\\paragraph{Few-NERD (NER).}\nFew-NERD \\cite{fewnerd-2021} provides 66 fine-grained entity types organized into 8 coarse categories (person, location, organization, building, art, product, event, other). The dataset spans diverse domains, making it suitable for cross-domain evaluation. We sample 1{,}000 test instances uniformly across test instances; type frequency follows the natural long-tail distribution of Few-NERD. Evaluation uses token-level text matching. Pairwise analysis covers 1{,}953 type pairs with non-zero co-occurrence out of $\\binom{66}{2}=2{,}145$ possible pairs.\n\n\\paragraph{FewRel (RE).}\nFewRel \\cite{fewrel-2018} contains 80 relation types sourced from Wikidata. Since the validation split covers only 16 relations, we combine the training and validation splits to obtain the full 80-relation pool required for schema-scale experiments, and sample 1{,}000 balanced instances (12--13 per relation type, stratified to ensure equal representation). Each instance provides a sentence with marked head and tail entities; the task is to classify the relation.\n\n\\paragraph{DocRED (Document-level RE).}\nDocRED \\cite{docred-2019} contains 96 relation types sourced from Wikidata, evaluated at the document level where entity pairs may span multiple sentences. We sample 1{,}000 instances balanced across 96 relation types ($\\sim$10 per type) from the DocRED development set and formulate the task identically to FewRel: given a document with marked head and tail entities, classify the relation. DocRED extends the RE evaluation to a larger schema (96 vs.\\ 80 types) and document-level context, testing whether confusability effects persist under longer input contexts and cross-sentence reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{MAVEN (ED).}\nMAVEN \\cite{maven-2020} defines 168 event types derived from the FrameNet ontology, making it a same-domain schema. We use the validation set (8{,}042 sentences containing 18{,}904 event mentions) for evaluation, as test set labels are not publicly available. The task requires both trigger identification and event type classification.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:datasets} summarizes the dataset statistics, including the Schema Confusability Score (SCS) ranges defined in \\S\\ref{sec:scs}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrlll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Dataset} & \\textbf{Task} & \\textbf{\\# Types} & \\textbf{\\# Eval Units} & \\textbf{Domain} & \\textbf{BCS Range} & \\textbf{Embedding} \\\\\n\\midrule\nFew-NERD & NER & 66 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.77--0.93 & Qwen3-32B \\\\\nFewRel & RE (sent.) & 80 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.42--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\nDocRED & RE (doc.) & 96 & 1{,}000 & Cross & 0.53--0.73 & MiniLM \\\\\nMAVEN & ED & 168 & 18{,}904\\rlap{$^\\dagger$} & Same & 0.87--0.97 & MiniLM \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Dataset statistics. BCS Range indicates the min--max Batch Confusability Scores across count-curve batches (Experiment~1). Embedding column indicates the model used for SCS/BCS computation. $^\\dagger$Trigger mentions across 8{,}042 sentences in 710 documents.}\n\\label{tab:datasets}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Schema Confusability Score (SCS)}\n\\label{sec:scs}\n\nWe introduce the Schema Confusability Score to quantify semantic overlap between types in a schema. Given a type $t$ with name string $\\text{name}(t)$, an embedding function $\\text{embed}(\\cdot)$ maps the type name to a vector representation. The \\textbf{pairwise SCS} between types $t_i$ and $t_j$ is:\n{\\small\n\\[\n\\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j) = \\cos\\bigl(\\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_i)),\\; \\text{embed}(\\text{name}(t_j))\\bigr)\n\\]\n}\n\nFor a batch of types $B = \\{t_1, \\ldots, t_k\\}$, the \\textbf{Batch Confusability Score (BCS)} aggregates all pairwise similarities:\n\\[\n\\text{BCS}(B) = \\frac{1}{\\binom{k}{2}} \\sum_{i < j,\\; t_i, t_j \\in B} \\text{SCS}(t_i, t_j)\n\\]\n\nWe compute SCS with two embedding models: (1)~Qwen3-32B \\cite{qwen3-2025} hidden-state representations (5{,}120-d), which capture the extraction model's own type representations, and (2)~all-MiniLM-L6-v2 \\cite{minilm-2020,sentence-bert-2019}, a 384-dimensional general-purpose sentence encoder. SCS uses name-only embeddings as a practical simplification. We validate this choice empirically: name+definition SCS yields equivalent predictive accuracy ($\\Delta r$: $-0.023$ to $+0.035$; Appendix~\\ref{app:scs-ablation}), and for MAVEN/FewRel prompts that use type names without definitions, name-only SCS directly matches prompt content. We use MiniLM as the primary embedding for BCS computation because sentence-transformers are specifically trained for semantic similarity \\cite{sentence-bert-2019}; a cross-embedding sensitivity check using Qwen3-32B causal-LM hidden states confirms directional consistency but with compressed BCS range (std${}\\,=\\,0.034$ vs.\\ MiniLM std${}\\,=\\,0.081$), consistent with known autoregressive anisotropy \\cite{ethayarajh-contextual-2019}.\n\n\\paragraph{SCS as a lightweight proxy.}\n\\label{sec:scs-validation}\nSCS measures embedding-space similarity rather than task-grounded confusion, making it a pre-hoc proxy computable without running extraction experiments. Empirical validation confirms the proxy is informative: pairwise SCS correlates with observed type confusion on Few-NERD ($r = 0.112$, $p < 0.001$) and FewRel ($r = 0.187$, $p < 0.001$). However, SCS is a partial proxy---context, type frequency, mention ambiguity, and definition quality also contribute to confusion (\\S\\ref{sec:pairwise}). Task-grounded confusion scores derived from empirical error matrices would capture these factors but require running extraction first, forfeiting the predictive utility that makes SCS useful for schema design.\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Design}\n\\label{sec:exp_design}\n\nWe conduct two complementary experimental paradigms.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 1: Count Curves.}\nWe vary the number of types presented in the prompt schema to measure performance as a function of schema size. For each count level, we sample 5 random type batches and evaluate the same 1{,}000 test samples across all batches. At count${}=1$, every type is evaluated individually as a solo baseline. The count levels follow natural partition points in each schema's type hierarchy rather than uniform spacing: \\{1, 2, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for Few-NERD, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 80\\} for FewRel, \\{1, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 96\\} for DocRED, and \\{1, 6, 12, 22, 44, 64\\} for MAVEN.\n\n\\paragraph{Experiment 2: BCS-Controlled.}\nWe fix type count and vary BCS to isolate confusability from schema size. For each count level, we construct one low-BCS batch, one high-BCS batch, and 25 random batches (27 total). We evaluate on Few-NERD (count$\\,\\in\\{6, 12\\}$), FewRel (count$\\,\\in\\{8, 16\\}$, both 16-relation and 80-relation pools), and DocRED (count$\\,\\in\\{16, 32\\}$). Within-pool 16-relation subsets from the 80-relation pool isolate confusability from pool composition. MAVEN results are reported separately due to a centrality confound (\\S\\ref{sec:centrality_confound}).\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}\n\\label{sec:metrics}\n\n\\textbf{NER}: text-match F1---a prediction is correct if the exact text string matches after lowercasing and whitespace normalization, \\emph{and} the type matches; partial spans count as incorrect. \\textbf{RE}: accuracy---exact match of predicted relation label to gold. \\textbf{ED}: relaxed F1---any token overlap between predicted and gold trigger spans, with correct event type.\n\n\n\\subsection{Model and Inference}\n\\label{sec:model}\n\nWe use Qwen3-32B-FP8 \\cite{qwen3-2025} served via vLLM v0.20.2 \\cite{vllm-2023} on a single 96\\,GB GPU with greedy decoding (temperature${}\\,=\\,0$, seed 42). Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation; variance across seeds is not quantified. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation; variance across seeds is not quantified. All metrics include 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).",

Two NER/ED prompt versions are evaluated: v1 requests character offsets (evaluated via text matching); v2 adds format constraints, doubling precision with minimal recall loss. RE uses a single prompt version throughout. Both versions produce consistent degradation patterns; we report both where applicable to quantify formatting sensitivity \\cite{sclar-sensitivity-2024} (templates in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix_prompts}). Cross-model validation uses Meta-Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\cite{llama3-2024}. All experiments use single-seed evaluation; variance across seeds is not quantified. Pair-level CIs use standard percentile bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$); batch-level CIs use $B{=}1{,}000$ due to computational cost."

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MVEAdh2nEmqSSvsNPNreZD`</summary>

```text
=== 31.2 occurrences (prose, excluding table cells) ===
introduction.tex:12:Our experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two empirically separable channels---a confusability channel with a typical-batch gap of ${\approx}\,2.2$\,pp (quartile $\Delta = -5.3$\,pp, 95\% CI: [$-$7.4, $-$3.2]; up to 31.2\,pp under extreme-batch construction), and a count channel associated with recall decline up to $-$49\,pp; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification, but RE batch composition yields a directional +1.4\,pp gain.
results.tex:56:In BCS-controlled FewRel subsets, the high-BCS quartile shows $\Delta=-5.3$\,pp lower accuracy than low-BCS (quartile comparison; full data in Appendix~\ref{app:bcs-controlled-table}). At 80 relations, extreme-batch construction amplifies this to $-$31.2\,pp (CI: [17.7, 42.7]), stable across count levels. An intermediate count${}\,=\,40$ evaluation (40-relation subsets sampled from the 80-relation pool) yields consistent gaps ($-$33.3\,pp at count 8, $-$31.8\,pp at count 16), confirming monotonic decline.
results.tex:67:For representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\,pp (95\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\% vs.\ 78.9\% for top-quartile ($\Delta = 3.9$\,pp). The 31.2\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Pair-level $R^2 \approx 7\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\ $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\%$ across all pairs; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \approx 22\%$ ($\sim$3$\times$ amplification, reproduced by Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation-sim}).

=== 2.2 occurrences ===
abstract.tex:4:We identify two empirically separable degradation channels: count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type substitution. For RE, the quartile BCS gap is $-$5.3\,pp (95\% CI: [$-$7.4, $-$3.2]); typical random batches differ by ${\approx}\,2.2$\,pp. Under extreme-batch construction, the gap widens substantially (Table~3). Batch-level BCS explains $R^2 = 0.22\text{--}0.53$ of accuracy variance as an aggregated signal, while pairwise effect sizes remain modest ($r^2 = 1.3\text{--}1.7\%$). The effect is directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts. Channel~1 replicates fully on Llama-3.1-8B; Channel~2 replicates on RE but shows floor effects on 8B NER. Count increases generally suppress extraction on cross-domain schemas; MAVEN's same-domain schema exhibits centrality confounding that reverses this trend. Schema decomposition via oracle routing recovers count-based degradation for RE (+29.8\,pp over single-pass), but confusability-aware grouping yields no significant additional gain (+1.4\,pp, $p\approx 0.19$).
discussion.tex:11:Since typical random batches differ by only ${\approx}\,2.2$\,pp (\S\ref{sec:practical_magnitude}), the practical impact depends on schema composition.
discussion.tex:30:We isolated schema-induced degradation into count-driven recall decline and confusability-driven type confusion---typical batches differ by ${\approx}\,2.2$\,pp; quartile gap $-$5.3\,pp---showed these channels are separable in RE and replicate across model families (Llama-3.1-8B: count $r = 0.725$, confusability $\Delta = -27$\,pp) and extraction granularities (DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$). We further identified a centrality confound on same-domain schemas and demonstrated that merge amplification is a structural barrier to NER decomposition, while RE batch composition yields a directional +1.4\,pp gain ($p\approx 0.19$). Cross-batch calibration and broader model validation are key next steps.
introduction.tex:12:Our experiments yield five findings: (1)~degradation operates through two empirically separable channels---a confusability channel with a typical-batch gap of ${\approx}\,2.2$\,pp (quartile $\Delta = -5.3$\,pp, 95\% CI: [$-$7.4, $-$3.2]; up to 31.2\,pp under extreme-batch construction), and a count channel associated with recall decline up to $-$49\,pp; (2)~pairwise SCS predicts type confusion ($r = 0.112$--$0.129$), the first factor empirically isolated in controlled zero-shot IE; (3)~effects are consistent across schema sizes and granularities (BCS slopes: $p = 0.60$; DocRED: slope $= -0.71$, $R^2 = 0.53$), and directionally consistent across prompt designs, with attenuated magnitude under improved prompts; (4)~per-type aggregation is confounded by centrality on homogeneous schemas; (5)~NER decomposition fails due to merge amplification, but RE batch composition yields a directional +1.4\,pp gain.
results.tex:67:For representative batches from the 80-relation pool ($n{=}100$ random 16-type subsets), the typical BCS span (IQR = 0.054) yields an expected accuracy gap of 2.2\,pp (95\% CI: [0.4, 4.0])---roughly 7\% of the extreme-batch gap. Quartile comparison confirms: bottom-quartile BCS batches average 82.8\% vs.\ 78.9\% for top-quartile ($\Delta = 3.9$\,pp). The 31.2\,pp figure reflects maximum BCS contrast, not the typical batch experience. Pair-level $R^2 \approx 7\%$ among non-zero confusion pairs (vs.\ $r^2 = 1.3$--$1.7\%$ across all pairs; Table~\ref{tab:pairwise}) aggregates to batch-level $R^2 \approx 22\%$ ($\sim$3$\times$ amplification, reproduced by Monte Carlo simulation accounting for inter-pair correlation structure; Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation-sim}).

=== TOST/equivalence ===
checklist.tex:11:Yes. The Limitations section (after \S\ref{sec:conclusion}) discusses single-model reliance, modest pairwise effect sizes, cross-pool slope consistency, factorial interaction tests with TOST equivalence, decomposition scope, and metric scope.
main.tex:44:\item \textbf{Factorial interaction tests.} Within-dataset count $\times$ BCS interaction on Few-NERD did not reach significance ($p = 0.50$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}). However, a FewRel factorial with TOST equivalence testing confirms additive separability ($p_{\text{TOST}} = .003$, interaction = 0.47\,pp; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}), and cross-pool slope consistency ($p = 0.60$) corroborates stable rather than amplifying sensitivity.
main.tex:160:A linear model with count, BCS, and their interaction as predictors explains $R^2 = 0.52$ of accuracy variance. Both main effects are significant (count: $p = .008$; BCS: $p = .0002$), but the interaction term is not ($\Delta = 0.47$\,pp, $p > 0.05$). A TOST equivalence test with a $\pm 5$\,pp margin confirms that the interaction is negligibly small ($p = .003$), supporting an additive (separable) model of count and confusability effects on RE accuracy.
main.tex:251:\caption{BCS--accuracy regression under mean vs.\ max pairwise SCS aggregation (FewRel, 16-relation batches). Permutation test for slope equivalence across pools: MEAN $p \approx 0.60$; MAX $p \approx 0.44$. The 80-pool row is identical under both definitions because these batches were collected using mean-aggregated BCS; the MAX column reflects the same data for completeness.}
results.tex:87:These results establish two channels: \textbf{Channel~1} (count predicts recall decline) and \textbf{Channel~2} (confusability predicts type confusion), cleanly separable in RE but coupled in NER where type ambiguity and boundary ambiguity interact. A FewRel factorial with TOST equivalence testing formally confirms additive separability (interaction = 0.47\,pp, $p_{\text{TOST}} = .003$; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}).
